All participants listened to the same standardized comments in the same order.

At the end of the study, participants were asked who they thought selected the praising and critical comments about them that the audio recordings were based on (response options for parents: Partner, Child, Both, None; response options for children: Mother, Father, Both, None).

2.3. Procedure.

Parents provided written informed consent, and children provided assent to participate in the study. The project was approved by the Ethics Committee of the University of Antwerp (BUN B3002022000092) and carried out in accordance with the Declaration of Helsinki.

The data reported in this study are part of the first wave of the project, which consisted of three parts. In the first part, participants completed a one-hour online questionnaire at home, collected and managed using REDCap (Harris et al., 2009 (Harris et al., , 2019) ) and hosted at Ghent University. This questionnaire included demographic questions and psychological measures. In the second part, participants attended an in-person experimental session lasting approximately two hours, conducted at a participating school, community building, or university laboratory. Participants were instructed to abstain from eating, drinking (except water), or smoking for one hour before the session, and to refrain from vigorous physical activity for 12 hours prior to the session. Upon arrival, both the child and their parents received an overview of the session and signed informed consent (parents) and informed assent (children). Electrodes for ambulatory physiological monitoring were then attached. Participants were asked to wear noise-canceling headphones throughout the session to minimize distractions and maintain task focus. They were seated in separate locations across the room, with visual dividers separating them, and could not interact with each other during the session. Participants were then asked to complete Part I of the family criticism paradigm. A 10-minute habituation phase was then administered to obtain baseline psychophysiological measures, consisting of 5 min of rest followed by a 5-minute vanilla task in which participants watched a video of ocean waves and pressed the space bar each time a wave reached the shore. Participants were informed that performance would not be evaluated. As proposed by Laborde et al. (2017), vanilla tasks are useful to avoid mind-wandering and disruptive thoughts that may affect psychophysiological processes and compromise baseline measurements. Following the habituation phase, participants were then exposed to Part II of the family criticism paradigm, followed by a recovery period consisting of two 5-minute intervals during which they remained quietly seated. To conclude the session, height and weight were measured, and the families were debriefed. During the session, saliva samples were collected for hormone, peptides, and genetic assessment (data not reported here). See Figure 1 for a description of the protocol and measures included in this study. Finally, the third part of the study consisted of an online session in which participants completed the Middle Childhood Attachment Script Assessment (data not reported here).

Instructions, questionnaires, and tasks during the experimental session were presented using OpenSesame (Mathôt et al., 2012), accompanied by verbal instructions from the experimenters to ensure participants understood the instructions.

2.4. Measures.

>Measures.

2.5. Questionnaires during the laboratory session.

>Questionnaires during the laboratory session.

Mood.

Participants completed five Visual Analog Scales (VAS; McCormack et al., 1988) to assess changes in stress perception, tension, anger, sadness, and happiness during the session. The scales used a 10-centimeter line ranging from "not at all" to "very much." The responses from the participants were converted into a 0 to 100 scale. The VAS were completed (i) after the resting period, (ii) after the vanilla period, (iii) after the praising comments, (iv) after the critical comments, (v) after the end of the first 5-min recovery period, and finally (vi) after the second 5-min recovery period.

State rumination.

A state version of the Self-Critical Rumination Questionnaire (state-SCRQ; Smart et al., 2016; Allaert et al., 2025) was used to assess self-critical rumination during the experiment. The questionnaire consists of 10 items rated on a 4-point Likert scale ranging from "completely disagree" to "completely agree." The final score is computed as the sum of the 10 items. An example item is: "During the last five minutes, my attention was focused on aspects of myself that I'm ashamed of." Participants completed the questionnaire (i) after the resting period, (ii) after the vanilla period, (iii) after the first 5-minute recovery period, and (iv) after the second 5-minute recovery period.

2.5.1. Heart Rate Variability.

Cardiovascular activity and SCL were continuously recorded throughout the session using the Vrije Universiteit Ambulatory Monitoring System (VU-AMS; De Geus & Van Doornen, 1996), an ambulatory device that records electrocardiogram (ECG), thoracic impedance cardiography (ICG), and electrodermal activity. ECG and ICG were recorded from five electrodes (Covidien Kendall H135SG) placed on the participant´s chest and two on the participant´s back. A 1000 Hz sampling rate was used for the ECG and ICG recordings. SCL was recorded using two electrodes (GSR Electrodes SA2659) attached to the medial phalanges of the index and middle fingers of the non-dominant hand using a 10 Hz sampling rate, and a current between the two electrodes at a constant voltage of 0.5V. To compute HRV, PEP, and SCL, the data were imported to the VU-DAMS software (http://www.psy.vu.nl/vu-ams) for visual inspection, artefact correction, and R-to-R interval interpolation. Interbeat intervals time series were computed from the R-to-R distances after being manually corrected. As an index of vagally mediated HRV, we used the root mean square of successive difference (RMSSD), which reflects parasympathetic control and is relatively free of respiratory influences (Laborde et al., 2017). Higher RMSSD values indicate higher parasympathetic activation.

The PEP was computed as the time interval, in milliseconds, between the onset of ventricular depolarization (Q-wave onset in the ECG) and the onset of ventricular ejection (B-point of the dZ/dt waveform from the ICG). The Q-wave onset and the B-point were manually corrected when necessary.

When the Q-wave onset was missing, PEP was calculated as the B-point minus 41 ms. Lower PEP values indicate higher sympathetic activation. Regarding SCL, the measurement ranged from 1 to 100 μS, with a resolution of 0.0125 μS. Higher SCL values indicate greater sympathetic activation. OpenSesame (Mathôt et al., 2012) was used to register event markers.

Five-minute HRV epochs were calculated for the 5-min vanilla task (i.e., Baseline) and the two recovery periods to investigate parasympathetic recovery after criticism. To investigate acute responses to the comments during the family criticism paradigm, HRV was calculated for each 30second audio segment and for the subsequent 30-second silence period. For each emotional valence (neutral, praise, and criticism), this resulted in eight fragments: four 30-second comment fragments and four 30-second silence fragments. Previous research has demonstrated the reliability of 30s epochs for RMSSD as an index of HRV (Baek et al., 2015; Munoz et al, 2015). PEP and SCL were computed only for the 5-min vanilla task (baseline) and recovery periods. PEP was not calculated during the family criticism paradigm because the 30-second epochs were too short to reliably estimate this parameter (Kelsey, 2012). Similarly, SCL was not computed during the family criticism paradigm because the number, duration, and characteristics of the audio segments did not allow reliable differentiation between tonic SCL and superimposed phasic responses (skin conductance response), nor between successive phasic responses that may overlap in time (Boucsein et al., 2012).

2.6. Data management and statistical analyses.

For mood analyses, we used VAS scores collected at the end of the 5-min vanilla task, the positive and negative comments, and the two 5-min recovery periods. For self-critical rumination, we used scores collected at the end of the 5-min vanilla task and the two 5-min recovery periods. For analyses of HRV, PEP, and SCL before and after the family criticism paradigm, we used the 5-min vanilla task and the two 5-min recovery periods. For HRV analyses during the family criticism paradigm, we used the eight epochs (four 30-second audio fragments and four 30-second silence fragments) for each emotional valence (neutral, praise, and criticism).

T-tests and the chi-square test (or Fisher's exact test when the frequency of some cell was lower than five) were used to investigate sex differences in demographic characteristics. Linear mixed-effects models (LMM) were used to investigate changes in mood (VAS: Stress, Tension, Anger, Happiness, and Sadness), self-critical rumination, and autonomic activity (HRV, PEP, and SCL). For analyses with mood, self-critical rumination, and with HRV, PEP, and SCL before and after the family criticism paradigm, Time (Mood: Baseline, Praise, Criticism, 1 st Recovery, 2 nd Recovery; Self-critical rumination, HRV, PEP, SCL: Baseline, 1 st Recovery, 2 nd Recovery), and Sex (Male vs Female) were used as fixed effects. For analyses with HRV during the family criticism paradigm, Valence (Neutral, Praise, and Criticism), Fragment (Comment, Silence), and Sex (Male vs. Female) were used as fixed effects.

Subject was included as a random intercept in all LMMs. Stress, Tension, Sadness, Anger, SCRS, RMSSD, and SCL were log-transformed using log(x + 1) because the residual distributions were nonnormal. Happiness and PEP were analyzed on the raw scale as their residuals were normally distributed. P-values are provided using the Kenward-Roger degrees of freedom approximation. Data points with extreme values (±3SD) were excluded from the analyses (see Table S1 in the supplementary material for a description of the number of outliers).

Because developmental differences may affect stress perception and psychophysiological regulation, and adults and children show large differences in tonic (baseline) autonomic activity (Kudielka et al., 2004; Pulopulos et al., 2018; Thayer et al., 2021), we examined parents and children in separate models. Results of analyses that combine both parents and children in the same models are reported in the supplementary material. We also run a aeries of sensitivity analyses to test the robustnest of the findings. We repeated the analyses (i) using Family as a random intercept (for LMMs with parents and for LMMs with both parents and children together), (ii) using Valence as a random slope for analyses using HRV during the family criticism paradigm, (iii) including all the outlier datapoints, and (iv) including the factor Language (Dutch vs French) as a main factor and its interactions wioth the other factors in the models. The statistical conclusions of the study remain the same across these models (results reported in the Supplementary materials). Therefore, we report the simpler analyses using only Subject as a random intercept and excluding outlier datapoints in the main text.

Analyses were performed in R (version 4.5.0; R Core Team, 2025) in conjunction with RStudio (version 2025.05.0+496). The 'lme4' (Bates et al., 2015) package was used to compute LMM. The 'emmeans' package (Lenth & Piaskowski, 2025), with Tukey corrections for multiple comparisons, was used for follow-up analyses of significant main effects and interactions. The 'ggplot2' package (Wickham, 2016) was used to create graphs, and the 'MuMIn' package (Bartoń, 2025) was used to calculate the marginal r-squared (r 2 m) and conditional r-squared (r 2 c) as measures of effect size.

For power calculations for future studies, means and standard deviations are reported in the supplementary material.

3. Results.

>Results.

3.1. Missing data, outliers, characteristics of the sample, and preliminary analyses.

Complete self-report data (Mood and Self-critical rumination) could not be recorded for five mothers, six fathers, and seven children, and one child had missing data at baseline. Due to technical issues, cardiovascular data could not be recorded for three mothers, three fathers, and three children across different families. Additionally, PEP values could not be computed for the entire session for a further three mothers, one father, and three children due to signal noise in impedance cardiography, and SCL data could not be computed for the entire session for a further six mothers and one child.

HRV during the family criticism paradigm (30-sec segments) could not be computed for four mothers, two fathers, and two children. Finally, physiological data from certain phases of the laboratory session could not be computed for some participants due to signal noise (PEP: one mother, two fathers, and one child; SCL: two mothers, one father, and one child; HRV 30-sec: one child). A table detailing the missing data is presented in the Supplementary material (Table S2). Participants were excluded from analyses using variables with complete missing data. Sociodemographic characteristics of the sample are presented in Table 1. Mothers were younger (p<0.001), had a lower body mass index (p=0.022), and drank less alcohol units per week (p<0.001) than fathers. Among children, girls were more advanced in puberty than boys (p<0.001).

Older age was related to lower perceived stress during critical comments in mothers (r=-0.204, p=0.031) and to higher perceived stress during praise in children (r=0.315, p<0.001). No other correlations between age and the main outcomes were significant (p>0.052).

3.2. Mood.

>Mood.

3.2.1. Tension

>Tension

Parents

Results of the LMM showed that the factor Time was significant (F(4, 873.41)=76.77, p<0.001), whereas the main effect of Sex (F(1, 223.89)=1.28, p=0.259) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(4, 873.41)=1.25, p=0.289). Post hoc analyses indicated that parents reported higher tension after criticism than after any other phase (p<0.001). All other comparisons were not significant (p>0.289). The model explained an R²m of 0.133 and an R²c of 0.540. Results are shown in Figure 2A.

Children

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(4, 432.41)=28.25, p<0.001), whereas the main effect of Sex (F(1, 111.07)=0.18, p=0.674) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(4, 432.41)=0.65, p=0.626). Post hoc analyses indicated that children reported lower tension during praise than at baseline (p<0.001) and the 1st Recovery phase (p=0.002). Tension was also lower during the 2nd Recovery phase than at baseline (p=0.002). Tension levels were higher during criticism than during any other phase (p<0.001). Tension was also higher during the 1st Recovery phase than during the 2nd Recovery phase (p=0.006). All other comparisons were not significant (p>0.998). The model explained an R²m of 0.096 and an R²c of 0.560. Results are shown in Figure 2B.

3.2.2. Sadness

>Sadness

Parents

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(4, 870.27)=105.66, p<0.001), whereas the main effect of Sex (F(1, 224.80)=0.11, p=0.740) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(4, 870.27)=1.54, p=0.190). Post hoc analyses indicated that sadness was higher during criticism than during any other phase (p<0.001). Sadness during the 1st Recovery phase was also higher than at baseline (p<0.001), praise (p=0.003), and the 2nd Recovery phase (p=0.016). No other comparisons were significant (p>0.833). The model explained an R²m of 0.169 and an R²c of 0.569. Results are shown in Figure 2C.

Children

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(4, 432.66)=123.17, p<0.001), whereas the main effect of Sex (F(1, 110.97)=1.16, p=0.283) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(4, 432.66)=2.04, p=0.088). Post hoc analyses indicated that sadness was higher during criticism than during all other phases (p<0.001). Sadness during the 1st Recovery phase also remained higher than at baseline, praise, and the 2nd Recovery phase (p<0.001). Sadness during the 2nd Recovery phase was also higher than during praise (p=0.003). No other comparisons were significant (p>0.108). The model explained an R²m of 0.365 and an R²c of 0.608. Results are shown in Figure 2D.

3.2.3. Anger

>Anger

Parents

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(4, 881.69)=106.29, p<0.001), whereas the main effect of Sex (F(1, 224.61)=0.01, p=0.921) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(4, 881.69)=0.57, p=0.684). Post hoc analyses indicated that anger was higher during criticism than during all other phases (p<0.001). Anger was also lower during praise than at baseline and the two Recovery phases (p<0.004). All other comparisons were not significant (p>0.088). The model explained an R²m of 0.183 and an R²c of 0.525. Results are shown in Figure 2E.

Children

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(4, 429.81)=139.51, p<0.001), whereas the main effect of Sex (F(1, 111.00)=0.03, p=0.870) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(4, 429.81)=0.34, p=0.851). Post hoc analyses indicated that anger was higher during criticism than during all other phases (p<0.001). Anger during the 1st Recovery phase was also higher than at baseline (p<0.001), praise (p<0.001), and the 2nd recovery period (p<0.029). Anger during the 2nd Recovery phase was higher than at baseline (p=0.007) and praise (p<0.001). All other comparisons were not significant (p>0.290). The model explained an R²m of 0.423 and an R²c of 0.596. Results are shown in Figure 2F.

3.2.4. Happiness

>Happiness

Parents

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(4, 897.23)=322.28, p<0.001), whereas the main effect of Sex (F(1, 225.00)=0.26, p=0.608) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(4, 897.23)=1.78, p=0.131). Post hoc analyses indicated that happiness was higher during praise than during all other phases (p<0.001), and lower during criticism than at baseline and both recovery phases (p<0.001). Parents reported lower happiness during both recovery phases than at baseline (p<0.006), whereas the two recovery phases did not differ (p=0.806). The model explained an R²m of 0.360 and an R²c of 0.687. Results are shown in Figure 2G.

Children

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(4, 438.83)=172.06, p<0.001), whereas the main effect of Sex (F(1, 110.90)=0.60, p=0.440) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(4, 438.83)=0.34, p=0.850). Post hoc analyses indicated that happiness was higher during praise than during all other phases (p<0.001), and lower during criticism than at baseline and both recovery phases (p<0.001). Children also reported lower happiness during the 1st and 2nd

Recovery phases than at baseline (p<0.003), and happiness during the 2nd Recovery phase was higher than during the 1st Recovery phase (p=0.011). The model explained an R²m of 0.469 and an R²c of 0.627. Results are shown in Figure 2H.

3.2.5. Stress

>Stress

Parents

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(4, 873.59)=50.41, p<0.001), but the main effect of Sex (F(1, 223.80)=0.46, p=0.500) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(4, 873.59)=1.71, p=0.146). Post hoc analyses indicated that participants reported more stress during criticism than during any other phase of the experiment (p<0.001). Stress levels were higher during praise than during the 2nd Recovery phase (p=0.025). All other comparisons were not significant (p>0.170). The model explained an R²m of 0.092 and an R²c of 0.520. Results are shown in Figure 2I.

Children

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(4, 430.40)=20.94, p<0.001), but the main effect of Sex (F(1, 111.06)=2.43, p=0.122) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(4, 430.40)=1.28, p=0.278). Post hoc analyses indicated that children reported higher stress at baseline than during praise (p<0.001) and both recovery phases (p<0.045). Stress levels were also higher during criticism than during praise (p<0.001) and both recovery phases (p<0.001). Praise also showed lower stress than the 1st Recovery phase (p=0.025). All other comparisons were not significant (p>0.186). The model explained an R²m of 0.082 and an R²c of 0.557. Results are shown in Figure 2J.

3.3. Self-critical rumination.

>Self-critical rumination.

Parents

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(2, 438.49)=93.41, p<0.001), whereas the main effect of Sex (F(1, 223.40)=0.03, p=0.864) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(2, 438.49)=1.62, p=0.198). Post hoc analyses indicated that self-critical rumination was higher during the 1st Recovery and 2nd Recovery phases than during habituation (p<0.001), and decreased from the 1st Recovery to the 2nd Recovery phase (p<0.001). The model explained an R²m of 0.134 and an R²c of 0.534. See Figure 3A.

Children

Results showed that the factor Time was significant (F(2, 216.95)=43.38, p<0.001), whereas the main effect of Sex (F(1, 110.99)=0.42, p=0.521) and the interaction between Sex and Time were not significant (F(2, 216.95)=1.27, p=0.284). Post hoc analyses indicated that self-critical rumination was higher during the 1st Recovery and 2nd Recovery phases than during habituation (p<0.001), and lower during the 2nd Recovery than during the 1st Recovery phase (p<0.001). The model explained an R²m of 0.117 and an R²c of 0.568. See Figure 3B.

3.4. HRV during the family criticism paradigm.

>HRV during the family criticism paradigm.

Parents

The results are presented in Figure 4A. Results showed significant main effects of Valence (F(2, 5223.8)=63.08, p<0.001) and Fragment (F(1, 5223.1)=66.04, p<0.001), and a significant interaction between Valence and Fragment (F(2, 5223.2)=9.35, p<0.001). The main effect of Sex was not significant (F(1, 230.0)=3.81, p=0.052). A significant interaction between Valence and Sex was observed (F(2, 5223.8)=5.20, p=0.006). No other interactions were significant (p>0.220). Post hoc analyses for the Valence factor indicated that HRV was higher during neutral audios than during praise and criticism (p<0.001). No differences in HRV between praise and criticism were observed (p=0.823).

HRV was also higher during silent fragments than during fragments when participants listened to the comments.

Follow-up analyses of the interaction between Valence and Fragment showed that during fragments when participants listened to the comments HRV was higher in neutral than in praise or criticism audios (p<0.001). No differences in HRV between praise and criticism were observed (p=0.248). During Silence fragments, HRV was again higher for neutral than for praising (p<0.001) and critical audios (p<0.001), whereas praising and critical audios did not differ (p=0.732). Examining fragment differences within each valence indicated that HRV was higher during silent fragments than during comments (when participants listened to the comments) for praising and critical audios (p<0.001), whereas the difference was not significant in neutral audios (p=0.154).

Finally, regarding the significant interaction between Valence and Sex, HRV was higher during neutral audios than during praise and criticism in both fathers (p<0.001) and mothers (p<0.001). No differences were observed between praise and criticism in both fathers and mothers (p>0.522). Sex differences within valence showed that women had higher HRV than men during praise (p=0.042) and criticism (p=0.023), whereas no sex differences were observed during neutral audios (p=0.139). The model explained an R²m of 0.021 and an R²c of 0.798.

Children

The results are presented in Figure 4B. In children, results showed significant main effects of Valence (F(2, 2671)=37.70, p<0.001), Fragment (F(1, 2671)=8.51, p=0.004), and Sex (F[1] (1, 115)=10.69, p=0.001). The interaction between Valence and Fragment was significant (F(2, 2671)=3.23, p=0.040). No other interactions were statistically significant (Valence x Sex: p=0.091; Fragment x Sex: p=0.287; Valence x Fragment x Sex: p=0.388). Post hoc analyses indicated that HRV was higher during neutral audios than during praise and criticism (p<0.001). No differences between praise and criticism were observed (p=0.170). HRV was also higher during silent than during comment fragments, and boys showed higher HRV than girls. Follow-up analyses of the Valence x Fragment interaction showed that HRV was higher during silence than during comment fragments for both praising (p=0.005) and critical audios (p=0.010), whereas the difference was not significant for neutral audios (p=0.699). Also, HRV was lower during praising and critical audios than during neutral audios for both silence and comment fragments (p<0.009). No differences were observed for neutral audios (p=0.699). The model explained an R²m of 0.075 and an R²c of 0.824.

3.5. HRV, PEP, and SCL before and after the family criticism paradigm.

3.5.1. Heart rate variability (HRV)

>Heart rate variability (HRV)

Parents

Results showed a main effect of Time (F(2, 448.27)=41.38, p<0.001). The effect of Sex (F(1, 227.00)=3.27, p=0.072) and the Time by Sex interaction were not significant (F(2, 448.27)=0.36, p=0.696). Post hoc analyses indicated lower HRV during habituation than during the 1st Recovery and 2nd Recovery phases (p<0.001), and an increase from the 1st Recovery to the 2nd Recovery phase (p=0.012). The model explained an R²m of 0.025 and an R²c of 0.902. See Figure 5A.

Children

Results showed a main effect of Time (F(2, 226.07)=19.65, p<0.001). The factor Sex (F(1, 114.00)=12.06, p=0.001) was also significant, with boys showing higher HRV than girls. The interaction between Time and Sex was not significant (F(2, 226.07)=0.66, p=0.516). Post hoc analyses indicated that HRV was higher during both recovery phases than during habituation (1st Recovery: p=0.001; 2nd Recovery: p<0.001), and HRV increased from the 1st to the 2nd Recovery phase (p=0.027). The model explained R²m = 0.099 and R²c = 0.905. See Figure 5B.

3.5.2. Pre-ejection period (PEP)

>Pre-ejection period (PEP)

Parents

Results showed a main effect of Time (F(2, 442.24)=4.42, p=0.013), with no main effect of Sex (F(1, 225.00)=2.19, p=0.141) and no Time by Sex interaction (F(2, 442.24)=1.94, p=0.145). Post hoc analyses indicated that PEP was shorter during the 2nd Recovery phase than during habituation (p=0.014). No differences in PEP were observed between habituation and the 1st Recovery phase (p=0.066), or between the 1st Recovery and 2nd Recovery phases (p=0.829). The model explained an R²m of 0.010 and an R²c of 0.940. See Figure 5C.

Children

Results showed no main effect of Time (F(2, 216.36)=0.007, p=0.993). The factor Sex (F(1, 110.95)=3.69, p=0.057), and the interaction between Time and Sex were also not significant (F(2, 216.36)=1.27, p=0.284). The model explained an R²m of 0.030 and an R²c of 0.857. See Figure 5D.

3.5.3. Skin conductance level (SCL)

>Skin conductance level (SCL)

Parents

Results showed no main effect of Time (F(2, 436.43)=0.07, p=0.932). The factor Sex was significant (F(1, 224.00)=9.72, p=0.002), with fathers showing higher SCL than mothers (p=0.002). The interaction between Time and Sex was not significant (F(2, 436.43)=2.69, p=0.069). The model explained an R²m of 0.040 and an R²c of 0.931. See Figure 5E.

Children

Results showed a main effect of Time (F(2, 222.20)=10.45, p<0.001), with no significant effect of Sex (F(1, 113.03)=0.24, p=0.628) and no Time by Sex interaction (F(2, 222.20)=0.38, p=0.681). Post hoc analyses showed that SCL was higher during both recovery phases than during habituation (p<0.001). No differences in SCL were observed between the 1st Recovery and 2nd Recovery phases (p=0.856). The model explained an R²m of 0.007 and an R²c of 0.919. See Figure 5F.

3.3. Perceived source of the critical and praise comments 1

>Perceived source of the critical and praise comments 1

Parents

There were no significant sex differences in the perceived source of criticism (χ²(3) = 1.94, p = 0.584).

Among fathers, 52 (46.8%) believed the partner selected the comments, 13 (11.7%) the child, 26 (23.4%) both, and 20 (18.0%) none. Among mothers, 47 (42.0%) believed the partner selected the comments, 18 (16.1%) the child, 22 (19.6%) both, and 25 (22.3%) none. Similarly, regarding the perceived source of praise, there were no significant sex differences (Fisher's exact test, p = 0.939).

Among fathers, 31 (27.9%) believed the partner selected the comments, 15 (13.5%) the child, 62 (55.9%) both, and 3 (2.7%) none. Among mothers, 27 (24.1%) believed the partner selected the comments, 15 (13.4%) the child, 67 (59.8%) both, and 3 (2.7%) none.

Children

There were no significant sex differences on the perceived source of criticism (χ²(3)=2.16, p=0.539). Among boys, 13 (21.0%) believed their mother selected the comments, 16 (25.8%) their father, 21 (33.9%) both parents, and 12 (19.4%) none. Among girls, 11 (22.4%) believed their mother selected the comments, 8 (16.3%) their father, 22 (44.9%) both parents, and 8 (16.3%) none.

Regarding praise, there were also no significant sex differences (Fisher's exact test, p = 0.201). Among boys, 19 (30.6%) believed their mother selected the comments, 10 (16.1%) believed their father selected them, 33 (53.2%) believed both parents selected them, and no participants selected "None".

1 Data regarding the perceived source of the praise and criticism was missing for seven mothers, eight fathers, and nine children. The statistical conclusions for tension, anger, sadness, happiness, and HRV remain the same if the analyses are performed including only participants who answered "none" to the question regarding the source of the critical comments.

Among girls, 8 (16.3%) believed their mother selected the comments, 8 (16.3%) their father, 32 (65.3%) both parents, and 1 (2.0%) none.

4. Discussion

The present study introduced and evaluated a paradigm for investigating psychophysiological responses to family-framed evaluative feedback. Overall, the findings show that the paradigm effectively elicited consistent, mild-to-moderate emotional, cognitive, and autonomic responses in both parents and children. Across family members, exposure to positive comments increased happiness compared to habituation, whereas negative comments consistently increased self-reported tension, sadness, and anger, while reducing happiness, relative to habituation and praise. The same effect was observed among parents regarding stress perception, whereas in children, stress levels increased only after criticism compared to praise. In both parents and children, happiness remained lower during the 10-minute period following criticism than during the habituation phase. The affective responses were paralleled by increases in self-critical rumination during recovery, suggesting sustained cognitive processing following the paradigm. At the autonomic level, criticism and praise were associated with lower parasympathetic cardiac activity (HRV) than neutral segments, whereas parasympathetic cardiac activity was lower during comment listening than during silent fragments, indicating dynamic regulatory shifts. Regarding the recovery period, parents showed higher parasympathetic control and reduced sympathetic cardiac activity (as observed in PEP) compared with baseline. In children, higher parasympathetic control was also observed during the last 5 min of the recovery period, accompanied by higher peripheral sympathetic activity, as reflected in SCL.

Social evaluative threat is known to elicit affective and autonomic responses, particularly when feedback concerns core aspects of the self (Bosch et al., 2009; Vanderhasselt et al., 2015). In the present study, negative comments framed as originating from family members consistently increased negative affect. Moreover, HRV was lower during praise and criticism compared to neutral segments, and higher during silence than during comments, indicating a dynamic reduction in parasympathetic activity during positive and negative social evaluation and relative recovery during brief pauses. These results are consistent with the idea that family-framed evaluative feedback engages processes within the central autonomic network (Smith et al., 2020). Following the neurovisceral integration framework, reduced vagally mediated HRV during evaluative input may reflect a temporary reduction of prefrontal inhibitory control over subcortical and limbic regions, facilitating affective reactivity (Smith et al., 2017; Thayer & Lane, 2009). The similar parasympathetic response to praise and criticism can be explained by the fact that, as suggested by recent reviews, both positive and negative affect trigger autonomic activation, leading to lower HRV due to the mobilization of metabolic resources due to demands and arousal (Gullet et al., 2023; Schneider et al., 2025). Importantly, because the comments were believed to have been selected by close family members, this design connects experimental stress paradigms and family research, which can be used to explore how evaluative dynamics within the family may trigger regulatory mechanisms crucial for stress resilience.

The integration of autonomic findings with self-critical rumination provides important insight into how the paradigm may activate perseverative cognitive and physiological processes. Self-critical rumination increased after the family criticism paradigm compared to the habituation period in both parents and children. Contrary to our hypothesis, HRV was higher during recovery than during habituation in both groups, indicating higher parasympathetic activity. In adults, this pattern was accompanied by a modest increase in PEP during the second recovery period, indicating reduced sympathetic cardiac activity mediated by the beta-adrenergic system and further supporting a shift toward increased parasympathetic cardiac control. In contrast, in children, no changes in PEP were observed, but an increase in SCL indicates higher general arousal, regulated by the acetylcholine system. However, note that when the analyses were performed with parents and children together, the changes in PEP over time did not differ between parents and children (see Supplementary material). Together, these findings indicate that although the paradigm increases self-critical rumination, it also triggers a parasympathetic rebound during recovery, whereas sympathetic changes during recovery are age-and peripheral-system-specific (adrenergic vs. acetylcholinergic systems). As hypothesized by the Vagal Tank Theory (Laborde et al., 2018), elevated HRV during a recovery period, with increased rumination, may reflect adaptive, momentary self-regulatory effort. Developmentally, the less coordinated parasympathetic and sympathetic responses observed in children (i.e., increased HRV and SCL activity during recovery) may reflect the ongoing maturation of autonomic regulatory systems (Fry et al., 2022; Harteveld et al., 2021). These coordinated yet partially dissociable autonomic dynamics highlight the value of a multi-system approach for understanding stress regulation within family interactions. Most importantly, this paradigm may be used to further investigate the influence of family-related processes (e.g., family resilience, attachment, interpersonal coping mechanisms) on individual differences in autonomic coordination.

Importantly, the psychophysiological reaction elicited by the paradigm was largely independent of sex, with only limited sex-specific differences observed in discrete domains (e.g., tonic SCL in parents and tonic HRV in children). When the analyses are repeated for parents and children together, the results confirm the same pattern of results, except for PEP, where no effects are observed (see Supplementary material). These analyses also confirm important tonic (baseline) differences in autonomic activity and show higher mood responses to criticism in children than in parents. Moreover, the results were observed in both French-speaking and Dutch-speaking families (see Supplementary material). Together, these findings support the validity of the paradigm for eliciting psychophysiological reactions and demonstrate that standardized, family-framed evaluative feedback reliably activates coordinated affective, cognitive, and autonomic stress systems in both parents and children. Some developmental differences should be considered when interpreting and using this paradigm. Whereas parents showed clear increases in self-reported stress from habituation to criticism, children did not exhibit the same increase, despite showing robust changes in tension, sadness, anger, and happiness. One possible explanation is that the construct of "stress" may be more abstract and cognitively demanding for children aged 10 to 12 years, who may differentiate less clearly between stress and discrete emotions (Hoemann et al., 2019). Alternatively, children may experience criticism more immediately in affective terms (e.g., sadness, anger) rather than labeling it as stress per se. This pattern underscores the importance of considering developmental differences in emotion regulation and self-report interpretation when using subjective stress measures in younger populations. Notably, children nevertheless showed clear affective and autonomic modulation, suggesting that the paradigm effectively elicited stress-related processes, even if the semantic label "stress" did not fully capture their experience.

The paradigm relied on participants believing that the praising and critical comments were based on the evaluations previously provided by their family members. Eighteen percent of the children and 20.2% of parents reported not believing that the critical comments were based on the selections made by other family members, whereas only 0.9% of the children and 2.7% of the parents expressed such doubts regarding the praising comments. This asymmetry between critical and praising comments may suggest that the question used to assess belief in the cover story did not adequately capture participants' actual engagement with the manipulation, was too coarse to fully capture credibility and relational salience, or that participants' responses reflected factors other than their genuine belief (e.g., post hoc reasoning). This interpretation is supported by the fact that when analyses were repeated, including only participants who did not believe that the critical comments were based on their family members' selections, the pattern of results remained largely consistent across affective, cognitive, and parasympathetic outcomes, despite the substantially reduced sample size (18% of the children and 20,5% of the parents). No effects were observed at the sympathetic level.

However, these results may also indicate that, among participants who do not believe the comments were selected by their family members, the emotional content of the comments was still sufficient to elicit psychophysiological responses, as observed in rumination and mood by Bonduelle et al. (2023).

This paradigm can be used in future research investigating synchrony in psychophysiological reactions to stress within families. Although not the main goal of the current study, exploratory correlation analyses with psychophysiological reactions to the paradigm among the three family members indicate modest positive correlations between family members' emotional responses at specific time points, most consistently between mothers and children (results reported in supplementary material, see Table S7). Future studies using this paradigm may provide important evidence on how behavioral-physiological responses interact and synchronize among family members at the dyadic and triadic level, and how developmental and family-related factors shape these associations.

Despite the important methodological contribution, a limitation of this study is that while HRV, PEP, and SCL were measured during the habituation and recovery periods, PEP and SCL could not be computed during the family criticism paradigm (when participants are listening to the audios) due to the methodological restrictions of these physiological indices (i.e., minimum time length for reliable assessment and difficulty in differentiating tonic from phasic responses during the audio segments).

Given that the PNS and SNS operate on different time scales and may respond to distinct aspects of evaluative processing (Berntson et al., 1991 (Berntson et al., , 1994)), the parasympathetic cardiac withdrawal during the family criticism paradigm does not necessarily imply a sympathetic activation. Future research using additional indices, such as continuous blood pressure or pupillometry, could further clarify the temporal coordination of stress systems during the phase when participants were listening to the audio. Moreover, the paradigm was tested in mother-father-child biological families with children aged 10-12 years, which provides a well-controlled family structure but limits the generalizability of the conclusions to single-parent families, blended families, same-sex parent families, adoptive families, and families with broader age ranges. Finally, participants always listened to praising comments first, then critical ones, aligning with previous studies (Baeken et al., 2018; Dedoncker et al., 2019) and with affective contrast theory, which states that emotional information has a stronger effect when presented after contrasting content (Manstead et al., 1983). This order allows investigating reactivity to praise and both reactivity and recovery from criticism as a social stressor.

Thus, the rise in self-critical rumination may be triggered by critical comments, since they came last, but it could also be due to overall paradigm exposure, which activates self-referential processes. Also, because criticism was always last, physiological recovery effects might reflect responses to criticism, but also end-of-task relief or uncertainty resolution. Although we recommend keeping this order fixed, future studies are needed to investigate whether counterbalancing the order of praise and criticism yields similar psychophysiological responses.

In conclusion, the present study provides initial validation of a standardized family criticism paradigm for investigating psychophysiological stress processes across family members. The paradigm combines experimental control with relational salience by exposing participants individually to prerecorded comments and framing them as selected by family members. This design addresses limitations of live family conflict tasks, which may introduce variability and interpersonal contagion, and standardized social stress tasks, which often lack relational relevance. Our findings show that social evaluation within the family context engages coordinated affective and regulatory mechanisms in both parents and children. This approach offers a reproducible method for studying family stress and may help future research clarify how repeated evaluative interactions contribute to resilience or vulnerability.

Open Practices Statement

3. Combined Analyses: Parents and Children

To examine whether stress responses differed between parents (mothers and fathers combined) and children, a set of additional linear mixed-effects models (LMM) were estimated in which parents and children were analyzed together. The fixed-effects structure was: outcome ~ Time × Sex × Role + (1 | Subject_ID), where Role indicates whether the participant was a parent or a child.

As in the analyses for parents and children separately, the Time factor levels for mood were Baseline, Praise, Criticism, 1st Recovery, and 2nd Recovery. The Time factor levels for SCRS were Baseline, 1st

Recovery, and 2nd Recovery. For HRV 5m, PEP, and SCL, Time factor levels were Baseline, 1st Recovery, and 2nd Recovery. For HRV 30-sec, Time factor levels were Valence (Neutral, Praise, Criticism) × Fragment (Comment, Silence). All models included Subject as a random intercept. Outlier detection was performed separately for parents and children (|z|>3 on the raw scale within each Role × Epoch cell) to account for substantial baseline differences between parents and children. Variables with nonnormally distributed residuals (Stress, Tension, Sadness, Anger, SCRS, RMSSD, and SCL) were logtransformed using log(x + 1). Happiness and PEP were analyzed on the raw scale.

phase was also higher than during praise (p=0.004). No significant differences were observed between baseline and praise, or between baseline and the 2nd Recovery phase (p>0.051). The Time × Role interaction showed that parents reported higher sadness than children during praise (p=0.028), while children reported higher sadness than parents during criticism (p<0.001) and during the 1st Recovery phase (p<0.001). No significant differences between parents and children were observed during baseline or the 2nd Recovery phase (p>0.342). No differences in sadness during praise and the 2nd recovery period were observed for parents (p=0.999). All the other within-role Time comparison remian similar to the ones described in the main effect of Time. Regarding the Time × Sex interaction, females reported higher sadness than males during the 1st Recovery phase (p=0.005), with no other significant Time-specific sex differences (p>0.149). The model explained an R²m of 0.253 and an R²c of 0.587.

Anger

Results showed significant main effects of Time (F(4, 1312.37)=272.38, p<0.001) and Role (F(1, 336.63)=27.08, p<0.001), and a significant Time × Role interaction (F(4, 1312.37)=33.53, p<0.001). The main effect of Sex and all the other interactions were not significant (p>0.756). The Role main effect indicated higher levels of anger in children than in parents overall. Post hoc analyses of the main effect of Time indicated that anger was higher during criticism than during all other phases (p<0.001). Anger during both recovery phases was also higher than at Baseline (p<0.001) and praise (p<0.001). Anger at baseline was higher than during praise (p=0.002), and the 1st Recovery phase showed higher anger than the 2nd Recovery phase (p=0.003). Follow-up analyses of the Time × Role interaction showed that children reported higher anger than parents during criticism (p<0.001), the 1st Recovery phase (p<0.001), and the 2nd Recovery phase (p=0.005). No significant differences were observed at baseline or during praise (p>0.824). In parents, anger was higher during criticism than during all other phases (p<0.001). Anger during both recovery phases was also higher than praise (p<0.001). Anger at baseline was higher than during praise (p=0.011). In children, anger was higher during criticism than during all other phases (p<0.001). Anger during both recovery phases was also higher than at Baseline (p<0.001) and praise (p<0.001). The 1st Recovery phase showed higher anger than the 2nd Recovery phase (p=0.006). No other comparisons were statistically significant (p=0.143). The model explained an R²m of 0.317 and an R²c of 0.572.

Happiness

Results showed significant main effects of Time (F(4, 1336.27)=460.0, p<0.001) and Role (F(1, 336.37)=34.45, p<0.001), and a significant Time × Role interaction (F(4, 1336.27)=23.99, p<0.001). The main effect of Sex (F(1, 336.37)=0.77, p=0.382) and all other interactions were not significant (p>0.313). The Role main effect indicated higher levels of happiness in children than in parents overall.

Post hoc analyses showed that happiness was higher during praise than during all other phases (p<0.001). Happiness was higher at baseline than during criticism (p<0.001) and both recovery phases (p<0.001). Both recovery phases showed higher happiness than criticism (p<0.001), and the 2nd

Recovery phase was higher than the 1st Recovery phase (p=0.003). The Time × Role interaction indicated that children reported higher happiness than parents at baseline (p<0.001), during praise (p<0.001), and during both recovery phases (p<0.001). No significant differences were observed during criticism (p=0.104). In parents, no significant differences were observed during the 1st and 2nd recovery periods (p=0.852). All the other differences followed the same pattern observed for the main effect of Time. The model explained an R²m of 0.422 and an R²c of 0.675.

Stress

Results showed significant main effects of Time (F(4, 1304.30)=53.84, p<0.001) and Role (F(1, 335.26)=4.27, p=0.040), and a significant Time × Role interaction (F(4, 1304.30)=8.68, p<0.001). The main effect of Sex was not significant (F(1, 335.26)=3.11, p=0.079), nor were the Sex × Role (F(1, 335.26)=1.05, p=0.306), the Time x Sex (F(4, 1304.30)=2.21, p=0.067), or the three-way interaction (F(4, 1304.30)=0.74, p=0.564). The Role main effect indicated higher stress levels in children than in parents overall. Post hoc analyses of the main effect of Time showed that stress was higher during criticism than during all other phases (p<0.001). Stress levels were also higher at baseline than during praise (p<0.001), the 1st Recovery phase (p=0.039), and the 2nd Recovery phase (p<0.001). No other significant differences were observed (p>0.057). Follow-up analyses of the Time × Role interaction showed that children reported higher stress than parents at baseline (p<0.001) and the 1st Recovery phase (p=0.011), whereas no significant differences were observed during praise, criticism, or the 2nd

Recovery phase (p>0.142). Parents reported higher stress during criticism than during all the other phases (p<0.001). In children, however, stress levels during the habituation period were not significantly different from stress levels during criticism (p=0.343). Children reported higher levels of stress at Baseline and during criticism than during all the other phases (p=0.030). The model explained an R²m of 0.094 and an R²c of 0.537.

3.2. Self-Critical Rumination (SCRS)

Results showed significant main effects of Time (F(2, 655.79)=129.02, p<0.001) and Role (F(1, 334.79)=23.76, p<0.001). The main effect of Sex and all the other interactions were not significant (p>0.092). The Role main effect indicated higher self-critical rumination in children than in parents overall. Post hoc analyses indicated that self-critical rumination was higher during both recovery phases than at baseline (p<0.001), and higher during the 1st than the 2nd Recovery phase (p<0.001).

The model explained an R²m of 0.160 and an R²c of 0.567.

3.3. Heart Rate Variability during the family criticism paradigm

Results showed significant main effects of Valence (F(2, 7894.5)=90.87, p<0.001), Fragment Post hoc analyses showed that HRV was higher during neutral segments than during praise and criticism (p<0.001), and during silent fragments than during comments. The Valence × Fragment interaction showed that HRV was higher during silence than during comment listening for praise and criticism (p<0.001), but not for neutral segments (p>0.154). The main effect of Role showed that children had higher HRV than parents. The Fragment × Role interaction indicated that the difference between silence and comments was observed in both in parents and children (p<0.001).

The Sex × Role interaction showed that boys showed higher HRV than girls (p<0.001), while no significant sex differences were observed among parents (p=0.056). The Valence × Sex × Role interaction confirmed the effect of Valence for boys vs girls and fathers vs mothers (Neutral comments: p>0.154; Praise and Criticism: p<0.001). In children, boys showed higher HRV than girls regardless of Valence (p<0.021), whereas in parents, mothers showed higher HRV than fathers during praise and criticism (p<0.046), but not during neutral comments (p=0.146). The model explained an R²m of 0.353 and an R²c of 0.870.

3.4. HRV, PEP, and SCL before and after the family criticism paradigm.

>HRV, PEP, and SCL before and after the family criticism paradigm.

Heart Rate Variability (HRV)

Results showed significant main effects of Time (F(2, 674.29)=55.86, p<0.001), Sex (F(1, 340.94)=4.24, p=0.040), and Role (F(1, 340.94)=232.78, p<0.001), and a significant Sex × Role interaction (F(1, 340.94)=16.45, p<0.001). The Time × Role interaction (F(2, 674.29)=0.34, p=0.709) and other interactions were not significant (p>0.497). Post hoc analyses indicated that HRV was higher during both recovery phases than at baseline (p<0.001), and during the 2nd Recovery than the 1st

Recovery phase (p<0.001). The main effects of Role and Sex reflected higher HRV in children than in parents, and in males (fathers and boys) than in females (mothers and girls). The Sex × Role interaction showed that boys reported higher HRV than girls among children (p<0.001), while no significant sex differences were observed among parents (p=0.084). The model explained an R²m of 0.423 and an R²c of 0.941.

Pre-Ejection Period (PEP)

Results showed a significant main effect of Role (F(1, 336.01)=53.58, p<0.001), with parents showing longer PEP than children. The main effect of Time (F(2, 658.49)=1.74, p=0.176), Sex (F(1, 336.01)<0.01, p=0.961), and all interactions were not significant (p>0.058). The model explained an R²m of 0.138 and an R²c of 0.938.

Skin Conductance Level (SCL)

Results showed significant main effects of Time (F(2, 658.58)=6.70, p=0.001), Sex (F(1, 336.97)=4.89, p=0.028), and Role (F(1, 336.97)=76.95, p<0.001), and a significant Time × Role interaction (F(2, 658.58)=7.64, p=0.001). Other interactions were not significant (p>0.128). The Role and Sex main effects reflected higher SCL in parents than in children, and in males (fathers and boys) than in females (mothers and girls) overall. Post hoc analyses indicated that SCL was higher during both recovery phases than at baseline (1st Recovery: p=0.003; 2nd Recovery: p=0.007). No significant differences between recovery periods were observed (p=0.966). The Time × Role interaction showed that in children, SCL was higher during both recovery phases than at baseline (1st Recovery: p<0.001; 2nd Recovery: p<0.001), while in parents, SCL did not differ significantly across phases (p>0.941).

No significant differences between recovery periods were observed (p>0.850). The model explained an R²m of 0.199 and an R²c of 0.940.

4. Sensitivity Analyses

All models reported in the main text and in the preceding supplementary sections used

Subject as a random intercept, with outliers exceeding ±3 SD within each epoch (or valence condition for HRV-30 min) removed prior to analysis. To evaluate the robustness of our conclusions, we re-ran these analyses with the following variations: (1) using Family as a random intercept; (2) a by-subject random slopes for Valence in the analyses with HRV during the criticism paradigm; (3) including outlier datapoints; and (4) including Language (Dutch vs.

French) as a fixed factor and its interaction with other factors. Below, for each variation, we report only the effects whose statistical conclusion changed relative to the original model. For all other effects, the statistical conclusions were identical to those reported for the original models.

4.1. Family as a random intercept.

Because parents and children were nested within families, we re-ran all parent-only and combined (parents and children) models with subjects nested within family (1 | Family/Subject_ID).

Family membership accounted for 0-28% of the random + residual variance (largest for SCL and Table S5. Statistical conclusions that changed when outlier data points are not excluded.

Without outlier exclusion, the main effect of Sex became nonsignificant.

HRV (30-sec) -Parents, Sex.

The marginal main effect of Sex reached significance without outlier exclusion, with mothers showing higher HRV than fathers.

pattern intact. Adding Language to the models revealed that Dutch-speaking families showed generally higher levels of tension, happiness, rumination, SCL, and HRV than French-speaking families, with two Language-dependent differences in tension and parental happiness. We therefore retain the simpler original model specification in the main text and interpret these small variations as supporting the robustness of the reported effects.

5. Synchrony in psychophysiological reactions between family members

This paradigm can be used in future research investigating synchrony processes in psychophysiological reactions to stress within families. Thus, we examined whether family members showed correlated emotional and physiological responses to the family criticism paradigm (Table S7).

We computed Pearson correlations between delta scores (epoch score minus baseline) for each variable and time point across three family member pairs (father-mother, father-child, and motherchild).

A total of 96 correlations were computed. Nine correlations (9.3%) were statistically significant, all positive in direction, indicating that when one family member showed a larger change from baseline, the other tended to as well. For self-report measures, significant correlations were observed for fathers and mothers (r=0.37, p=0.001) and mothers and children (r=0.24, p=0.020) in anger during praise. Additional significant correlations were found for tension between fathers and mothers after praise (r=0.25, p=0.012) and between fathers and children during the second recovery phase (r=0.24, p=0.019). Fathers and mothers also showed synchronous changes in happiness after criticism (r=0.21, p=0.033). For sadness, father-child synchrony was observed during the first recovery phase (r=0.27, p=0.007), and mother-child synchrony after criticism (r=0.20, p=0.045). No significant synchrony was found for stress at any time point or for any dyad. For physiological measures, synchrony was limited to SCL: mothers and children showed correlated SCL changes during both the first (r=0.23, p=0.022) and second (r=0.25, p=0.013) recovery phases. No significant synchrony was observed for HRV (5-min), PEP, or HRV (30-sec).

Overall, family synchrony was modest in magnitude, specific to particular time points and dyads. Synchrony was absent for physiological measures other than SCL, suggesting that shared emotional responding was not paralleled by coordinated autonomic activity. However, given the large number of explorative analyses, these results should be interpreted with caution.

Puis tu as fait la queue pour la payer et tu as aussi acheté un paquet de chewing-gum au comptoir. Tout le trajet a duré moins d'une heure. 3. One of the things you did today was to go grocery shopping with us. You walked to the supermarket to get the things on her grocery list. We wanted to make dinner so you bought some eggs, some cheese, and some tomatoes. You also bought milk, orange juice and some cereal for breakfast. When you got home you brought the groceries to the kitchen and then watched TV. Eén van de dingen die je vandaag gedaan hebt, is boodschappen gaan doen met ons. Je stapte naar de supermarkt om de dingen op het boodschappenlijstje te kopen. Je moeder/vader avondeten maken, dus kocht je eieren, kaas en tomaten. Je kocht ook melk, fruitsap en ontbijtgranen. Toen je thuiskwam, bracht je de boodschappen naar de keuken en keek je daarna naar tv. Une des choses que tu as faites aujourd'hui, c'est d'aller faire les courses avec nous. Tu as marché jusqu'au supermarché pour acheter les choses sur la liste de courses. Nous voulions préparer le souper, alors tu as acheté des œufs, du fromage et des tomates. Tu as aussi acheté du lait, du jus d'orange et des céréales pour le déjeuner. En rentrant à la maison, tu as apporté les courses dans la cuisine et tu as regardé la télévision. 4. One of the things you did this week was to go to the mall with us. You wanted to buy a present for a friend of yours. Because it was a week-day afternoon, the mall wasn't especially crowded, so it didn't take you very long to buy the present. Then you went into several stores and did some window shopping. You tried on clothes in one store but didn't wind up buying anything. Eén van de dingen die je deze week gedaan hebt, is naar het winkelcentrum gaan met ons. Je wou een cadeau kopen voor een vriend van je. Omdat het een weekdag was, was het er niet zo druk, dus hoefde je niet lang aan te schuiven bij de kassa om het cadeau te kopen. Daarna ging je verschillende andere winkels binnen en keek je naar de etalages. Je paste kleren in één winkel maar kocht uiteindelijk niets. Une des choses que tu as faites cette semaine a été d'aller au centre commercial avec nous. Tu voulais acheter un cadeau pour un de tes amis. Comme c'était un après-midi de semaine, le centre commercial n'était pas particulièrement bondé, et il ne vous a pas fallu beaucoup de temps pour acheter le cadeau. Tu es ensuite entré dans plusieurs magasins pour faire du lèche-vitrine. Tu as essayé des vêtements dans un magasin, mais tu n'as rien acheté. 5. One of the things you did today was to spend some time outside in the fresh air. The weather was quite mild for the time of year. You noticed that there were a lot of clouds in the sky but it didn't seem as if it was going to rain. Although it was fairly quiet, you were aware of the sound of other people as well as the sound of traffic in the distance. Eén van de dingen die je vandaag gedaan hebt, is buiten wat tijd doorbrengen in de frisse lucht. Het weer was vrij zacht voor de tijd van het jaar. Je merkte op dat er veel wolken waren, maar toch leek het er niet op dat het ging regenen. Hoewel het vrij rustig was, was je je bewust van het geluid van andere mensen en van het verkeer in de verte. L'une des choses que tu as faites aujourd'hui a été de passer un peu de temps dehors à l'air frais. Le temps était plutôt doux pour cette période de l'année. Tu as remarqué qu'il y avait beaucoup de nuages dans le ciel mais qu'il ne semblait pas qu'il allait pleuvoir. Bien que ce soit assez calme, tu étais conscient du bruit des autres personnes ainsi que du bruit de la circulation au loin. 6. One of the things you decided to do today was to clean your room. First you tidied up your closet and cleared the floor of your stuff. Then you put your dirty clothes in the laundry bin and emptied the trash bin in your room. After a short break you also tidied up your desk and you put your toys and books in the right place. By the time you were finished you went to the kitchen. Eén van de dingen die je vandaag beslist hebt te doen, is je slaapkamer opruimen. Eerst ruimde je je kleerkast op en maakte je de vloer leeg. Daarna deed je je vuile kleren in de wasmand en maakte je de vuilnisbak in je kamer leeg. Na een korte pauze ruimde je ook je bureau op en legde je je speelgoed en boeken op de juiste plaats. Toen je klaar was, ging je naar de keuken. L'une des choses que tu as décidé de faire aujourd'hui était de nettoyer ta chambre. Tu as d'abord rangé ton armoire et débarrassé le sol de tes affaires. Puis tu as mis tes vêtements sales dans le bac à linge et vidé la poubelle de ta chambre. Après une courte pause, tu as également rangé ton bureau et tu as mis tes jouets et tes livres à la bonne place. Quand tu as eu fini, tu es allé à la cuisine.

7. Another thing you did today was to arrange some of the papers in your school bag. A lot of the paper handouts were in there loosely and needed to be filed in the right folder. You also noticed that there were some dogeared scrap paper in there, so you were able to throw those out. The last thing you did was to make a list of the handouts that you needed to copy from one of your friends.

Iets anders dat je vandaag gedaan hebt, is dat je de papieren in je boekentas hebt geordend. Er zaten veel losse werkblaadjes in, die je in de juiste mappen moest steken. Je merkte ook op dat er een aantal verfrommelde kladblaadjes bij waren, dus deze kon je weggooien. Het laatste dat je gedaan hebt, is een lijstje maken van de werkblaadjes die je nog moest kopiëren van je vrienden.

Supplementary Material

The Family Criticism Paradigm: A Laboratory Task for Investigating Psychophysiological Responses in Parent-Child Triads

Tension

Results showed significant main effects of Time (F(4, 1306.12)=85.20, p<0.001) and Role (F(1, 335.33)=4.78, p=0.030), and a significant interaction between Time and Role (F(4, 1306.12)=4.88, p=0.001). The main effect of Sex (F(1, 335.33)=0.98, p=0.324) and all other interactions were not significant (p>0.382). The Role main effect indicated higher levels of tension in children than in parents overall. Post hoc analyses for the main effect of Time showed that tension was higher during criticism than during all other phases (p<0.001). Tension was also lower during praise than at Baseline (p=0.005) and the 1st Recovery phase (p=0.005). Both baseline and the 1st Recovery phase showed higher tension than the 2nd Recovery phase (p<0.001). No other significant differences were observed (p>0.969). Follow-up analyses of the Time × Role interaction showed that children reported higher tension than parents during baseline (p<0.001) and the 1st Recovery phase (p=0.002), whereas no significant differences were observed during praise, criticism, or the 2nd Recovery phase (p>0.247).

Both parents and children reported higher tension during criticism than during all the other phases (p<0.001). Children also reported higher levels of tension at baseline than during praise and the 2nd recovery period (p<0.001), and during the 1st recovery period than during praise (p<0.001). The model explained an R²m of 0.125 and an R²c of 0.551.

Sadness

Results showed significant main effects of Time (F(4, 1302.64)=235.50, p<0.001) and Role (F(1, 335.42)=4.21, p=0.041), a significant interaction between Time and Role (F(4, 1302.64)=19.41, p<0.001), and a significant interaction between Time and Sex (F(4, 1302.64)=2.85, p=0.023). The main effect of Sex (F(1, 335.42)=1.09, p=0.297) and other interactions were not significant (p>0.299). The Role main effect indicated higher levels of sadness in children than in parents overall. Post hoc analyses of the main effect of Time indicated that sadness was higher during criticism than during all other phases (p < 0.001). Sadness during the 1st Recovery phase was also higher than at Baseline (p<0.001), praise (p<0.001), and the 2nd Recovery phase (p<0.001). Sadness during the 2nd Recovery PEP). Only two fixed-effect tests changed their statistical conclusion. Both were borderline in the original models (Table S3).

4.4. Language (Dutch vs. French) as a fixed factor

We included Language (Dutch vs French) as a fixed factor to investigate its main effect and interaction with other factors in the models. In these new models, two original results become marginally significant (Sadness-Combined Role: .041 → .072; Sadness-Combined Epoch × Sex: .023 → .061). The Language main effect was significant in six models (Table S6), with Dutch-speaking participants showing higher values than French-speaking participants in all analyses (Table S6).

Two models showed a significant interaction between Language and other factors:

Tension -Combined, Epoch × Language: F(4, 1290.3) = 2.48, p = .043. Posthoc analyses showed that Dutch-speaking families reported more tension than French-speaking families at habituation (+0.41, p = .012), praise (+0.55, p < .001), and the 2nd recovery phase (+0.41, p = .013), but not during criticism (+0.08, p = .616) or the 1st recovery phase (+0.21, p = .205).

Happiness -Parents, Epoch × Language: F(4, 889.3) = 3.47, p = .008. Post hoc analyses showed that Dutch-speaking families reported more happiness than French-speaking families during criticism (+9.75, p = .005), the 1st recovery phase (+9.23, p = .008), and the 2nd recovery phase (+7.51, p = .030), but no differences were observed at habituation (p = .323) and praise (p = .794).

4.5. Summary of the sensitivity analyses

Across all four sets of variations, the substantive conclusions of the main analyses were preserved. The family random intercept and outlier-handling checks each moved only a small number of borderline effects across the significance threshold without reversing their direction. Random slope for Valence in HRV-30min analyses attenuated a single interaction while leaving the underlying 6.1. Comments for adults Criticism 1. One of the things that bothers me about you is how bad you are at dealing with negative feedback. If someone says anything even remotely critical of you, you tend to get very defensive. You are far from a perfect person -even if that is how you like to see yourself. I really wish you would listen when other people tell you what bothers them rather than getting all hostile and trying to defend yourself. Iets wat me stoort aan jou is hoe slecht je bent in het omgaan met negatieve feedback. Wanneer iemand nog maar iets kritisch over jou zegt, heb je de neiging om zeer defensief te reageren. Je bent verre van een perfect persoon -ook al is dat hoe je jezelf wil zien. Ik wou echt dat je luisterde naar anderen wanneer ze iets zeggen wat hen stoort, eerder dan dat je vijandig wordt en jezelf probeert te verdedigen. L'une des choses qui me dérange chez toi, c'est que tu ne sais pas gérer les critiques négatives. Si quelqu'un dit quoi que ce soit de critique à ton égard, tu as tendance à être sur la défensive. Tu es loin d'être une personne parfaite -même si c'est ainsi que tu aimes te voir. J'aimerais vraiment que tu écoutes les autres quand ils te disent ce qui les dérange, au lieu de te montrer hostile et d'essayer de te défendre. 2. One of the things that bothers me about you is that you sometimes gossip about people behind their backs. I know you don't mean to be bad but this is something that is really upsetting. When someone tells you something that is private, it isn't ok to share that with other people and to disclose another person's personal information. You may think it's harmless but people would be really upset if they found out. Een van de dingen die me aan jou stoort, is dat je soms roddelt over mensen achter hun rug. Ook al bedoel je het niet slecht, maak ik mij hier echt zorgen over. Als iemand je iets in privé vertelt, is het niet ok om dat te delen met andere mensen of dat door te vertellen. Je kan denken dat dit niet zo erg is, maar mensen zouden echt boos zijn, indien ze erachter zouden komen. Une des choses qui me dérange chez toi, c'est que tu racontes parfois des ragots sur les gens dans leur dos. Je sais que tu ne veux pas être méchant, mais c'est quelque chose qui me dérange vraiment. Quand quelqu'un te dit quelque chose de privé, ce n'est pas bien de le partager avec d'autres personnes et de divulguer les informations personnelles d'une autre personne. Tu peux penser que c'est inoffensif mais les gens seraient vraiment contrariés s'ils le découvraient. 3. One thing I don't like about you is how you never deliver on your promises. You will tell people you're going to do something and then it either never happens or you don't do it when you say you will. It's not ok to just do things when you feel like it. Sometimes, people are really relying on you. You need to follow up on things, even if it's inconvenient for you. in andere mensen. Het is een geweldige combinatie en het maakt mensen erg blij om in jouw buurt te zijn. Une chose que j'aime vraiment chez toi, c'est la façon dont tu fais attention aux gens avec qui tu es. Tu sembles vraiment capable de faire en sorte que les gens qui t'entourent se sentent bien. C'est en partie dû à ta capacité d'écoute. Mais tu as aussi une personnalité très chaleureuse et un véritable intérêt pour les autres. C'est une excellente combinaison qui rend les gens très heureux d'être en ta compagnie. 2. One thing I really like about you is how committed you are to the things you believe in. You really think about things and you seem to know what is truly important in life. You have a lot of insight and understanding about who you are as a person and I think that other people respect you for that. I really admire your integrity and the values you have as a human being. Iets dat ik echt leuk vind aan jou is hoe toegewijd je bent aan de dingen waar je in gelooft. Je denkt echt na over dingen en je lijkt te weten wat echt belangrijk is in het leven. Je hebt veel inzicht en begrip over wie je bent als persoon en ik denk dat andere mensen je hiervoor respecteren. Ik bewonder je integriteit en de waarden die je hebt als mens. Une chose que j'apprécie vraiment chez toi, c'est ton engagement envers les choses auxquelles tu crois. Tu réfléchis vraiment aux choses et tu sembles savoir ce qui est vraiment important dans la vie. Tu as beaucoup de perspicacité et de compréhension de qui tu es en tant que personne et je pense que les autres te respectent pour cela. J'admire vraiment ton intégrité et les valeurs que tu as en tant qu'être humain. 3. One of the things I really like about you is your sense of humor. It's not that you are always telling jokes or anything like that but you can be really, really funny. You have a quirky way of seeing the world that I just love. And when you laugh your whole face lights up and you're just this adorable person that everyone wants to be with. I just love to see you like that. Een van de dingen die ik echt in jou apprecieer is je gevoel voor humor. Het is niet zo dat je altijd grappen maakt of zo, maar je kan soms gewoon heel grappig zijn. Je hebt een originele kijk op de wereld, iets waar ik erg van hou. En wanneer je lacht dan licht je hele gezicht op. Je bent gewoon een heel aangename persoon in de omgang met mensen. Ik vind het ontzettend leuk om je zo te zien. Une des choses que j'aime vraiment chez toi, c'est ton sens de l'humour. Ce n'est pas que tu racontes toujours des blagues ou quelque chose comme ça, mais tu peux être vraiment, vraiment drôle. Tu as une façon excentrique de voir le monde que j'adore. Et quand tu ris, ton visage s'illumine et tu es juste une personne adorable avec qui tout le monde veut être. J'aime juste te voir comme ça. 4. One of the things I really like about you is how thoughtful you can be. You are really good at reading people's feelings and understanding what they are going through. You try very hard to be a good friend and you are so emotionally available to people. You're just…. you're a terrific person in that regard. Een van de dingen die ik echt in jou apprecieer, is hoe attent je kan zijn. Je bent goed in het aanvoelen en begrijpen van mensen. Je tracht steeds om een goede vriend te zijn, en je bent emotioneel steeds heel beschikbaar voor mensen. Je bent echt een bijzonder persoon in dat opzicht. L'une des choses que j'aime vraiment chez toi, c'est ta prévenance. Tu es très douée pour lire les sentiments des gens et comprendre ce qu'ils vivent. Tu t'efforces d'être une bonne amie et tu es très very caring and you can put yourself in the place of others emotions. And that's just something that is really wonderful about you. Iets dat ik echt leuk vind aan jou, is hoe goed je de gevoelens van anderen opmerkt. Je hebt een buitengewoon talent om de emoties van anderen te lezen, en te begrijpen wat ze doormaken. Je bent heel zorgzaam en je kunt jezelf goed verplaatsen in de gevoelens van anderen. En dat is gewoon iets fantastisch aan jou. Ce que j'aime vraiment chez toi, c'est ta capacité à percevoir les sentiments des autres. Tu as une capacité extraordinaire à lire les émotions des gens et à comprendre ce qu'ils vivent. Tu es très attentionné et tu peux te mettre à la place des émotions des autres. Et c'est quelque chose qui est vraiment merveilleux chez toi. 7. One of the things I really like about you is how loyal you are to your friends. You really care about the people you are close to and you always do what you can to make them feel important and special. Sometimes you show your caring in small ways. But your friends also know that, if they really need you, you will always be there to help them, no matter what. Your loyalty is something that people really value and admire about you. Eén van de dingen die ik echt leuk vind aan jou, is hoe trouw je bent aan je vrienden. Je zorgt echt voor de mensen die dicht bij jou staan en je doet altijd wat je kunt om ze zich belangrijk en speciaal te laten voelen. Soms toon je je zorg in kleine dingen. Maar je vrienden weten ook dat, als ze je echt nodig hebben, je er altijd zal zijn om hen te helpen, het maakt niet uit wat. Jouw trouw is iets dat mensen echt waarderen en bewonderen in jou. Une des choses que j'aime vraiment chez toi, c'est ta loyauté envers tes amis. Tu te soucies vraiment des personnes dont tu es proche et tu fais toujours ce que tu peux pour qu'elles se sentent importantes et spéciales. Parfois, tu montres ta sollicitude par de petits gestes. Mais tes amis savent aussi que, s'ils ont vraiment besoin de toi, tu seras toujours là pour les aider, quoi qu'il arrive. Ta loyauté est une chose que les gens apprécient et admirent vraiment chez toi. 8. Another thing I really like about you is the way that you think about things. Rather than taking the easy path and just going along with the crowd, you form your own ideas and you have your own opinions. You seem to be able to do this without trying too hard. You have a beautiful way of looking at the world. I think it reflects how thoughtful and creative you are. It also makes you much more interesting than most other children. Nog iets dat ik echt leuk vind aan jou, is de manier waarop je denkt over dingen. In plaats van de gemakkelijke weg te nemen en gewoon mee te lopen met de massa, vorm je je eigen ideeën en heb je je eigen mening. Je lijkt dit te kunnen zonder al te veel moeite. Je hebt een mooie manier om naar de wereld te kijken. Ik denk dat het weerspiegelt hoe behulpzaam en creatief je bent. Het maakt je ook veel interessanter dan de meeste andere kinderen Une autre chose que j'aime beaucoup chez toi, c'est la façon dont tu penses aux choses. Plutôt que de prendre le chemin le plus facile et de suivre la foule, tu te fais tes propres idées et tu as tes propres opinions. Tu sembles être capable de le faire sans trop te forcer. Tu as une belle façon de voir le monde. Je pense que cela reflète à quel point tu es réfléchi et créatif. Cela te rend également beaucoup plus intéressant que la plupart des autres enfants. Neutral

LegendsFigure 1.

Figure 1. Protocol and measures. HRV (30s) = Heart rate variability was calculated for each 30second comment and for the subsequent 30-second silence period. For each emotional valence (neutral, praise, and criticism), this resulted in eight fragments: four 30-second comment fragments and four 30-second silence fragments. HRV (5 min) = Heart rate variability was calculated for the 5min vanilla task and during the two recovery periods. PEP = Pre-ejection period; SCL = Skin conductance level.

6.

Comments used to the create the audio recordingsBelow are listed the comments used to create the Family Criticism Paradigm in English, Dutch (Belgian), and Fench (Belgian). Authors interested in using the version used in the current study can contact the first author to receive the audio recordings in Dutch (Belgian) and French (Belgian).

Cognitive Genetics (COGNET), Department of Medical Genetics, University of Antwerp, Belgium. Une autre chose que tu as faite aujourd'hui était de ranger certains papiers dans ton cartable. Un grand nombre de documents étaient éparpillés dans le sac et devaient être classés dans le bon dossier. Tu as également remarqué qu'il y avait des feuilles de papier brouillées, que tu as pu jeter. La dernière chose que tu as faite, c'est de faire une liste des documents que tu devais copier d'un de tes amis.

and spent most of the afternoon looking at that. When you had finished, you played a game, watched some TV, and then it was time for dinner. Iets dat je vandaag gedaan hebt, is een tijdje lezen. Het weer viel wat tegen, dus besliste je om thuis te blijven. Je vond een stripboek dat je al lang wou lezen en bracht het grootste deel van de namiddag daarmee door. Wanneer je hiermee klaar was, speelde je een game, keek je wat tv, en toen was het tijd om te eten.

Aujourd'hui, tu as notamment passé du temps à lire. Il ne faisait pas très beau dehors, alors tu as 'après-midi à la regarder. Lorsque tu as terminé, tu as joué à un jeu, regardé la télévision, puis il était temps de souper.
